# Chapter 6: Scale, maps and plans

## Chapter Overview
This chapter covers the practical application of scale in representing real-world objects and distances on paper. It includes the study of different types of scales, navigation, and the interpretation of architectural and spatial layouts.

---

## Table of Contents

| Section | Topic | Page |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

| 6.2 | Number and bar scales | 210 |
| 6.3 | Maps, directions, seating and floor plans | 224 |
| 6.4 | End of chapter activity | 232 |

---

## Key Concepts

### 1. Scale
Scale is the ratio of a distance on a map or plan to the corresponding distance on the ground. It allows large areas to be represented on a manageable surface.

### 2. Number Scales (Representative Fraction)
A number scale is expressed as a ratio, for example, $1 : 50\ 000$. This means that $1$ unit on the map represents $50\ 000$ of the same units in reality.

### 3. Bar Scales (Graphic Scale)
A bar scale is a line or bar drawn on a map that is divided into segments, showing the distance on the ground that each segment represents.

### 4. Maps and Directions
The study of using cardinal directions (North, South, East, West) and scale to determine the position of objects or locations relative to one another.

### 5. Seating and Floor Plans
The application of scale to architectural drawings, allowing for the calculation of actual room dimensions, furniture placement, and seating arrangements based on a scaled diagram.

# 6. Scale, Maps and Plans

## 6.1 Introduction and Key Concepts

Maps and plans are visual representations of the real world. They are tools used to navigate environments, locate specific places (such as shops in a mall), or identify positions (such as a seat in a stadium).

### Learning Objectives
In this chapter, you will learn how to:
* Use and understand **number scales** and **bar scales**.
* Understand the implications of resizing maps.
* Estimate actual distances using scale maps.
* Calculate scaled measurements based on actual distances.
* Read maps and seating plans to describe the position of objects.
* Find locations and follow directions between points.

### Definition: Scale
The scale of a map is a ratio of the distance on the map to the actual distance on the ground.

* **Example:** A number scale of $1 : 100$ means that $1$ unit on the map represents $100$ units in reality.
* Therefore: $1\text{ cm on the map} = 100\text{ cm} = 1\text{ m on the ground}$.

---

## 6.2 Number and Bar Scales

### The Number Scale
A number scale is expressed as a ratio, for example, $1 : 50$. 
* This means $1$ unit on the map represents $50$ units on the ground.
* **Example:** $1\text{ cm on the map} = 50\text{ cm on the ground}$.
* **Example:** $1\text{ m on the map} = 50\text{ m on the ground}$.

**How to use a number scale:**
1. Measure the distance on the map using a ruler.
2. Multiply that measurement by the "real" part of the scale ratio (e.g., $50$) to calculate the actual distance.

### The Bar Scale
A bar scale is a visual representation of distance. It provides a line segment marked with units that correspond to actual distances on the ground.

### Using Bar Scales on Maps

A bar scale represents a specific distance on the ground. To calculate real-world distances using a bar scale, follow these steps:

1.  **Measure the segment:** Use a ruler to measure the length of one segment on the bar scale (in cm).
2.  **Measure the map distance:** Measure the distance between two points on the map (in cm).
3.  **Calculate segments:** Divide the total map distance by the length of one segment:
    $$\text{Number of segments} = \frac{\text{Total map distance}}{\text{Length of one segment}}$$
4.  **Calculate real distance:** Multiply the number of segments by the distance value represented by one segment.

---

### Worked Example: Using Bar and Number Scales

#### Questions

1.  A map has a number scale of $1 : 40$. You measure a length of $10\text{ cm}$. What is the real-life distance?
2.  A map has a number scale of $1 : 500$. You measure a distance of $15\text{ cm}$. What is the real-life distance?
3.  Given the bar scale below, where you measure $15\text{ cm}$ on the map:
    

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 7" and the topic title "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It serves as an introductory signpost for a Mathematics or Technical Mathematics lesson, signaling the focus on geometric formulas and spatial calculations required by the CAPS curriculum.


    What is the actual distance?
4.  Given the bar scale below, where you measure $11\text{ cm}$ on the map:
    

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0002-01.png)

    What is the distance on the ground?

#### Solutions

**1. Number scale $1 : 40$**
*   Calculation: $10\text{ cm} \times 40 = 400\text{ cm}$
*   Conversion: $400\text{ cm} = 4\text{ m}$
*   **Result:** $4\text{ m}$

**2. Number scale $1 : 500$**
*   Calculation: $15\text{ cm} \times 500 = 7500\text{ cm}$
*   Conversion: $7500\text{ cm} = 75\text{ m}$
*   **Result:** $75\text{ m}$

**3. Bar scale (1 segment = $1,5\text{ cm}$ representing $50\text{ m}$)**
*   Segments measured: $\frac{15\text{ cm}}{1,5\text{ cm}} = 10\text{ segments}$
*   Real distance: $10 \times 50\text{ m} = 750\text{ m}$
*   **Result:** $750\text{ m}$

**4. Bar scale (1 segment = $2\text{ cm}$ representing $200\text{ m}$)**
*   Segments measured: $\frac{11\text{ cm}}{2\text{ cm}} = 5,5\text{ segments}$
*   Real distance: $5,5 \times 200\text{ m} = 1100\text{ m}$
*   Conversion: $1100\text{ m} = 1,1\text{ km}$
*   **Result:** $1,1\text{ km}$

### 6.2. Using number and bar scales to measure distance

In this section, we learn how to measure dimensions on scale maps and plans, and how to use number and bar scales to calculate the actual (real-world) dimensions of objects such as furniture and buildings.

---

### Activity 6 – 1: Using the bar and number scales

**1. Number Scale Calculation**
You measure the distance between two buildings on a map to be $5\text{ cm}$. If the map has a number scale of $1:100$, what is the actual distance on the ground?

**2. Number Scale Calculation**
You are given a map with the number scale $1:20$. You measure a distance of $12\text{ cm}$ on the map. What is the actual distance in real life?

**3. Bar Scale Calculation**
You measure a distance of $10\text{ cm}$ on a map with the following bar scale:


![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-00.png)

It functions as an organizational signpost to orient learners toward the core geometric concepts of quantifying two-dimensional surface area and three-dimensional space.


Given that $1\text{ cm} = 15\text{ m}$, what is the actual distance on the ground?

**4. Bar Scale Calculation**
You measure a distance of $15\text{ cm}$ on a map with the following bar scale:


![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-02.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0003-02.png)

### Worked Example 2: Using the number scale to estimate distance

#### Question
Study the school map provided and answer the questions that follow.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0004-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum resource, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce learners to the geometric principles and algebraic formulas required to determine the surface area and spatial capacity of various shapes.



**Scale:** $1 : 500$

1. Calculate the following real dimensions of the sports field in metres:
   a) length.
   b) width.
2. Calculate the length of the science classroom block in metres.
3. Zuki walks from the tuckshop to his maths classroom, along the blue dotted line shown. Measure how far he walked in metres.

---

#### Solution

**1. a) Calculating the real width of the sports field:**

*   **Step 1:** Measure the width of the sports field on the map using a ruler.
    *   Measurement = $5\text{ cm}$
*   **Step 2:** Use the number scale $1 : 500$ to determine the actual width.
    *   This means $1\text{ unit}$ on the map represents $500\text{ units}$ in reality.
    *   $\text{Actual width} = \text{Map measurement} \times 500$
    *   $\text{Actual width} = 5\text{ cm} \times 500 = 2\,500\text{ cm}$
*   **Step 3:** Convert the result to metres.
    *   Since $100\text{ cm} = 1\text{ m}$:
    *   $\text{Actual width} = \frac{2\,500}{100}\text{ m} = 25\text{ m}$

**Summary of Scale Calculation Method:**

| Step | Action | Formula |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 1 | Measure on map | $x\text{ cm}$ |
| 2 | Multiply by scale factor | $x \times 500$ |
| 3 | Convert to metres | $\frac{\text{Result}}{100}$ |

---

### Calculating Real Distances from Maps

To calculate the real-world distance from a map measurement, follow these steps:
1. **Multiply** your scaled measurement by the "real" number in the scale ratio.
2. **Convert** the resulting units (e.g., from cm to m) by dividing by the appropriate conversion factor (e.g., $100 \text{ cm} = 1 \text{ m}$).

#### Worked Examples

**Example 1: Calculating Field Dimensions**
*   **Width:**
    $5 \text{ cm} \times 500 = 2500 \text{ cm}$
    $2500 \text{ cm} \div 100 = 25 \text{ m}$
    The field is $25 \text{ m}$ wide.
*   **Length:**
    $10 \text{ cm} \times 500 = 5000 \text{ cm}$
    $5000 \text{ cm} \div 100 = 50 \text{ m}$
    The field is $50 \text{ m}$ long.

**Example 2: Science Classroom Building**
*   Map measurement: $5 \text{ cm}$
    $5 \text{ cm} \times 500 = 2500 \text{ cm}$
    $2500 \text{ cm} \div 100 = 25 \text{ m}$
    The science classrooms are $25 \text{ m}$ long.

**Example 3: Walking Distance**
*   Map measurement: $6 \text{ cm}$
    $6 \text{ cm} \times 500 = 3000 \text{ cm}$
    $3000 \text{ cm} \div 100 = 30 \text{ m}$
    Zuki walked $30 \text{ m}$ from the tuckshop to his maths classroom.

---

### Important Note on Accuracy
When measuring distance on a map to calculate real distance, you are **approximating or estimating**. Map measurements and scale conversions provide accuracy to the nearest metre or centimetre. In reality, physical measurements taken on the ground with a measuring tape may differ slightly from calculated estimates.

# Chapter 6: Scale, Maps, and Plans

## Understanding Scale
A scale is a ratio that compares the dimensions of a drawing or map to the actual dimensions of the object or area in real life. 

In the provided diagram, the scale is given as $1:500$. This means that $1$ unit of measurement on the map represents $500$ of the same units in reality.

*   **Example:** If a wall measures $2\text{ cm}$ on the map, the actual length of the wall in real life would be:
    $$2\text{ cm} \times 500 = 1000\text{ cm} = 10\text{ m}$$

## School Layout Plan
The following diagram represents a school layout plan drawn to a scale of $1:500$.

---

### Worked Example: Using a Bar Scale to Estimate Actual Length

#### Question
Study the map of the room provided and answer the questions that follow:



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0007-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 7" and the topic title "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It serves as an introductory signpost for a Mathematics or Technical Mathematics lesson, signaling the focus on geometric formulas and spatial calculations required by the CAPS curriculum.



1. Using a ruler and a calculator, calculate the following real lengths in metres:
    a) The length of the room.
    b) The length of the couch.

---

#### Solution

**Key Concept: Understanding the Bar Scale**
The bar scale provided indicates that a specific length on the map represents a specific distance in reality. In this example, the bar scale shows that $1\text{ cm}$ on the map represents $20\text{ cm}$ in reality.

**a) Calculating the length of the room**
1. Measure the length of the room on the map using a ruler.
2. The measurement is $15\text{ cm}$.
3. Determine the number of segments:
   $$\frac{15\text{ cm}}{1\text{ cm}} = 15\text{ segments}$$
4. Calculate the real length:
   $$15\text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 300\text{ cm}$$
5. Convert to metres:
   $$300\text{ cm} = 3\text{ m}$$
*The real length of the room is $3\text{ m}$.*

**b) Calculating the length of the couch**
1. Measure the length of the couch on the map using a ruler.
2. The measurement is $8\text{ cm}$.
3. Determine the number of segments:
   $$\frac{8\text{ cm}}{1\text{ cm}} = 8\text{ segments}$$
4. Calculate the real length:
   $$8\text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 160\text{ cm}$$
5. Convert to metres:
   $$160\text{ cm} = 1,6\text{ m}$$
*The real length of the couch is $1,6\text{ m}$.*

# Activity 6 – 3: Using the Bar Scale to Estimate Actual Length

## Concept: Using Bar Scales
A bar scale is a graphical representation of distance on a map or plan. It allows you to measure the distance on the drawing and convert it to real-life dimensions using the provided ratio.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0008-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an introductory signpost to orient learners toward the core geometric competencies required for calculating the spatial dimensions of 2D and 3D shapes.



### Instructions
Using the provided floor plan and the bar scale (where the black and white bar represents $20\text{ cm}$ per segment, totaling $40\text{ cm}$), calculate the real-life dimensions for the following items:

1. The length of the bookshelf.
2. The width and length of the chair.
3. The length of each of the windows.

---

## Understanding the Advantages and Disadvantages of Each Scale

### Overview
You have learned how to use number scales and bar scales to measure real-life dimensions and distances. However, it is important to consider what happens when a map or plan is resized (e.g., when making a photocopy of a map).

*   **Number Scales:** These are ratios (e.g., $1:100$). If you resize the document, the number scale becomes inaccurate because the physical size of the drawing changes, but the numbers in the ratio remain the same.
*   **Bar Scales:** These are generally more reliable when resizing. If you enlarge or shrink a map, the bar scale enlarges or shrinks proportionally with the rest of the drawing, maintaining the correct relationship between the drawing and the real-life distance.

### Worked Example 4: Resizing and Accuracy

#### Concept: Using Bar Scales
A bar scale is a graphical representation of a ratio. It allows you to measure the length of an object on a diagram and convert it to the actual size using the provided scale bar.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the labels "Chapter 7" and "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts required for learners to apply formulas to determine the surface area and capacity of various two-dimensional and three-dimensional shapes.



#### Question
1. Measure the width of the school bag in Diagram 1 and use the scale to calculate the width of the school bag.
2. Measure the school bag in Diagram 2 and use the scale to calculate the width of the school bag.
3. What do you notice about the answers for 1. and 2.?

---

#### Worked Solution

**Step 1: Measure and Calculate for Diagram 1**
*   **Measurement:** Using a ruler on the provided image, the width of the bag in Diagram 1 is approximately $2\text{ cm}$.
*   **Scale Reference:** The bar scale for Diagram 3 (corresponding to Diagram 1) shows that $1\text{ cm}$ on the diagram represents $15\text{ cm}$ in reality.
*   **Calculation:** 
    $$\text{Actual width} = \text{Measured width} \times \text{Scale factor}$$
    $$\text{Actual width} = 2\text{ cm} \times 15\text{ cm} = 30\text{ cm}$$

**Step 2: Measure and Calculate for Diagram 2**
*   **Measurement:** Using a ruler on the provided image, the width of the bag in Diagram 2 is approximately $3\text{ cm}$.
*   **Scale Reference:** The bar scale for Diagram 4 (corresponding to Diagram 2) shows that $1\text{ cm}$ on the diagram represents $10\text{ cm}$ in reality (calculated by observing the $15\text{ cm}$ marker is at $1.5\text{ cm}$ on the ruler).
*   **Calculation:**
    $$\text{Actual width} = 3\text{ cm} \times 10\text{ cm} = 30\text{ cm}$$

**Step 3: Observation**
*   **Notice:** Even though the bags are drawn at different sizes (different scales), the actual calculated width of the school bag remains the same ($30\text{ cm}$). This demonstrates that the scale is used to maintain the accuracy of the real-life dimensions regardless of the size of the drawing.

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0009-01.png]**

### Understanding Scale Resizing

When working with maps and diagrams, it is crucial to understand how resizing an image affects the accuracy of the scale.

#### Key Concepts
*   **Number Scale:** A ratio (e.g., $1 : 10$) that remains constant on the paper even if the image is enlarged or shrunk. This makes it unreliable for resized images because the physical distance on the paper changes, but the ratio does not.
*   **Bar Scale:** A graphical representation of distance. When a map is resized, the bar scale is resized proportionally along with the map, meaning it remains accurate regardless of the image size.

---

### Worked Example: Resizing a School Bag Diagram

**Problem:**
1. Measure the width of the school bag in Diagram 3 and use the scale to calculate the actual width.
2. Measure the school bag in Diagram 4 and use the scale to calculate the actual width.
3. Compare the results and explain the findings.

**Solution:**
*   **Diagram 3:** Measured width = $3\text{ cm}$. Using a scale factor of $15$:
    $$3\text{ cm} \times 15 = 45\text{ cm}$$
*   **Diagram 4:** Measured width = $5\text{ cm}$. Using the same scale factor of $15$:
    $$5\text{ cm} \times 15 = 75\text{ cm}$$

**Analysis:**
The answers are different because the diagram was resized. To keep the calculation accurate when using a number scale, the scale itself must be adjusted. When using a bar scale, the segments grow proportionally with the image, ensuring the calculation remains correct.

---

### Comparison: Number Scale vs. Bar Scale

| Feature | Number Scale | Bar Scale |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Accuracy when resized** | Becomes incorrect unless manually adjusted. | Remains accurate (resizes with the map). |
| **Ease of use** | Simple; only requires measuring one distance. | More complex; requires measuring segments and calculating how many fit into the distance. |

---

### Important Note on Map Resizing
If a map with a number scale of $1 : 10$ is printed at $10\text{ cm}$ wide, $1\text{ cm}$ on the map represents $10\text{ cm}$ on the ground. If you reprint that map so it is $15\text{ cm}$ wide, the number scale $1 : 10$ is no longer accurate because the physical representation of the distance has changed.

# Drawing a Scaled Map When Given Real Dimensions (EMG4V)

## Overview
A scale drawing represents a real-life object in a smaller, proportional size. While previous lessons focused on finding real measurements from a map, this section focuses on the reverse: determining scaled measurements to create an accurate two-dimensional map.

## Requirements for Drawing a Map
To draw an accurate map, you require two pieces of information:
1. **Actual Measurements:** The real-life dimensions of the objects to be mapped.
2. **Scale:** The ratio used to reduce the real-life size to the drawing size. 

*Note: In Grade 10, the scale will be provided to you.*

---

## Worked Example 5: Drawing Scaled Maps

### Question
Draw a scaled map of a room that has real dimensions of $3\text{ m}$ by $4,5\text{ m}$. Use a number scale of $1 : 50$.

### Solution
The scale $1 : 50$ means that $1$ unit on your drawing represents $50$ units in real life. Therefore, $1\text{ cm}$ on your drawing represents $50\text{ cm}$ in real life.

#### 1. Calculate the scaled width
*   **Convert real width to cm:**
    $$3\text{ m} \times 100 = 300\text{ cm}$$
*   **Calculate scaled width:**
    Divide the real measurement by the scale factor:
    $$300\text{ cm} \div 50 = 6\text{ cm}$$

#### 2. Calculate the scaled length
*   **Convert real length to cm:**
    $$4,5\text{ m} \times 100 = 450\text{ cm}$$
*   **Calculate scaled length:**
    Divide the real measurement by the scale factor:
    $$450\text{ cm} \div 50 = 9\text{ cm}$$

#### Final Result
The scaled measurements for your map are $6\text{ cm}$ by $9\text{ cm}$.

It functions as an introductory signpost to orient students toward the study of geometric formulas and spatial dimensions required for calculating the surface area and capacity of various shapes.



*Remember: Always include the scale on your final map.*

It illustrates the concept of calculating a perimeter or a combined length by summing individual segments, requiring students to account for an implicit missing value to satisfy the equality.

### Worked Example 6: Drawing a Scaled Map

#### Question
In this example, we add furniture to a room with dimensions $3\text{ m} \times 4,5\text{ m}$ using a scale of $1 : 50$. Draw the following items using the provided dimensions:

1. A couch: $2\text{ m} \times 1,2\text{ m}$
2. A window: $2\text{ m}$ long
3. A table: $1,5\text{ m}$ wide and $2\text{ m}$ long

#### Solution
The scale $1 : 50$ means that $1$ unit on your drawing represents $50$ units in real life. Therefore, $1\text{ cm}$ on your drawing represents $50\text{ cm}$ ($0,5\text{ m}$) in real life.

To find the scaled dimensions, divide the real-life dimensions by $50$ (or multiply by the scale factor).

| Item | Real Dimensions (m) | Calculation (Real $\div 50$) | Scaled Dimensions (cm) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Room | $3 \times 4,5$ | $300 \div 50$ and $450 \div 50$ | $6 \times 9$ |
| Couch | $2 \times 1,2$ | $200 \div 50$ and $120 \div 50$ | $4 \times 2,4$ |
| Window | $2$ | $200 \div 50$ | $4$ |
| Table | $1,5 \times 2$ | $150 \div 50$ and $200 \div 50$ | $3 \times 4$ |

*Note: The scaled dimensions of the room remain $6\text{ cm} \times 9\text{ cm}$. You may arrange the furniture within these boundaries in any sensible layout.*

---

### Calculating Scaled Dimensions
To convert real-world measurements to scaled dimensions using a scale of $1:50$, you must convert the real-world units to centimeters and then divide by $50$.

#### 1. Couch Dimensions
*   **Width:**
    *   Real width: $1,2\text{ m} = 120\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled width: $120\text{ cm} \div 50 = 2,4\text{ cm}$
*   **Length:**
    *   Real length: $2\text{ m} = 200\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled length: $200\text{ cm} \div 50 = 4\text{ cm}$
*   **Result:** The scaled dimensions of the couch are $2,4\text{ cm}$ by $4\text{ cm}$.

#### 2. Window Dimensions
*   **Length:**
    *   Real length: $2\text{ m} = 200\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled length: $200\text{ cm} \div 50 = 4\text{ cm}$
    *   *Note: The diagram indicates the scaled length of the window is $2\text{ cm}$ (representing $1\text{ m}$ in reality).*

#### 3. Table Dimensions
*   **Width:**
    *   Real width: $1\text{ m} = 100\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled width: $100\text{ cm} \div 50 = 2\text{ cm}$
*   **Length:**
    *   Real length: $1,5\text{ m} = 150\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled length: $150\text{ cm} \div 50 = 3\text{ cm}$
*   **Result:** The scaled dimensions of the table are $2\text{ cm}$ by $3\text{ cm}$.

---

### Activity 6 – 4: Drawing a scaled map

**Task:**
The bedroom in the picture is $3,5\text{ m}$ by $4\text{ m}$. It has a standard sized single bed of $92\text{ cm}$ by $188\text{ cm}$. The bedside table is $400\text{ mm}$ square. Draw a floor plan to show the layout of the room using a number scale of $1 : 50$.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0014-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0014-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0014-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum resource, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce learners to the geometric principles and algebraic formulas required to quantify the surface area and spatial capacity of various shapes.



**Instructions for calculation:**
To draw the map, convert all real-world measurements to the scaled measurement using the formula:
$$\text{Scaled length} = \frac{\text{Real length}}{\text{Scale factor}}$$

For a scale of $1 : 50$, divide the real-world dimensions by $50$. Ensure all units are converted to centimeters ($\text{cm}$) before calculating.

---

### Activity 6 – 5: Drawing scaled maps

**Task:**
Divide into groups to measure and draw an accurate, scaled floor plan of your classroom. Use a scale of $1 : 50$.

**Steps to follow:**
1. **Measure:** Measure all large objects in the classroom (e.g., desks, windows, the blackboard).
2. **Calculate:** Convert the real-world measurements into scaled dimensions using the scale $1 : 50$.
3. **Draw:** Carefully draw the floor plan on paper using your calculated scaled dimensions.
4. **Evaluate:** Consider if there is a different or better way to arrange the furniture in your classroom.

---

### Mathematical Reference: Scale Calculations

When working with a scale of $1 : n$, the relationship between the map distance ($d_m$) and the actual distance ($d_a$) is:

$$\frac{d_m}{d_a} = \frac{1}{n}$$

**Example Conversion Table (Scale $1 : 50$):**

| Object | Real Dimension | Calculation | Scaled Dimension |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Bedroom Length | $4\text{ m} = 400\text{ cm}$ | $400 \div 50$ | $8\text{ cm}$ |
| Bedroom Width | $3,5\text{ m} = 350\text{ cm}$ | $350 \div 50$ | $7\text{ cm}$ |
| Bed Length | $188\text{ cm}$ | $188 \div 50$ | $3,76\text{ cm}$ |
| Bed Width | $92\text{ cm}$ | $92 \div 50$ | $1,84\text{ cm}$ |
| Bedside Table | $400\text{ mm} = 40\text{ cm}$ | $40 \div 50$ | $0,8\text{ cm}$ |

# 6.3 Maps, Directions, Seating and Floor Plans

## Overview
Understanding how to use scale maps and describe the position of objects in relation to their surroundings is a vital skill. This includes:
*   Interpreting and writing directional instructions.
*   Reading seating plans and floor plans.
*   Working with number and bar scales.

---

## Worked Example 7: Using the number scale and directional navigation on a classroom seating plan

### Question
Study the classroom plan below and answer the questions that follow:



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0015-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a mathematics textbook, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and the topic "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts required for learners to calculate the surface area and capacity of various 2D and 3D shapes as prescribed by the CAPS curriculum.



### Analysis of the Plan
The classroom plan shows the layout of desks (labeled A to O), the teacher's desk, the blackboard, the door, windows, and a bookshelf. 

*   **Scale:** The plan uses a number scale of $1:50$. This means that $1$ unit on the map represents $50$ units in real life (e.g., $1\text{ cm}$ on the plan equals $50\text{ cm}$ in the actual classroom).

### Key Concepts for Navigation
When interpreting a floor plan:
1.  **Orientation:** Identify fixed points (e.g., the blackboard is at the front, the door is on the left).
2.  **Relative Position:** Use terms like "in front of," "behind," "to the left of," or "to the right of" to describe the location of specific desks.
3.  **Scale Calculation:** To find the real-world distance between two objects, use the formula:
    $$\text{Real distance} = \text{Map distance} \times \text{Scale factor}$$
    *Example:* If the distance between desk A and desk B on the plan is $2\text{ cm}$, the real distance is:
    $$2\text{ cm} \times 50 = 100\text{ cm} = 1\text{ m}$$

### Classroom Layout Reference Table

| Feature | Location/Description |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Front of Room** | Blackboard and Teacher's desk |
| **Left Wall** | Door and Windows |
| **Right Wall** | Bookshelf |
| **Desk Rows** | 5 rows (A-C, D-F, G-I, J-L, M-O) |

---

### Exercises
*Self-check questions based on the diagram:*
1. If you are sitting at desk **E**, describe your position in relation to the teacher's desk.
2. If you enter through the door, which desk is closest to you?
3. If the distance between desk **A** and desk **D** on the plan is $1.5\text{ cm}$, calculate the actual distance in the classroom using the $1:50$ scale. 
   *Calculation:* $1.5\text{ cm} \times 50 = 75\text{ cm}$.

### Classroom Seating Plan Analysis



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_Area-and-Volume.pdf-0016-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a Mathematics curriculum, featuring the text "Chapter 7" and "Measurement (Calculating Area and Volume)." It introduces the foundational geometric concepts of quantifying two-dimensional space and three-dimensional capacity, which are essential for learners to master spatial reasoning and practical measurement applications.



#### Exercises
1. How many learners are in this class if there is one learner at each desk?
2. Which learner is sitting to the left of learner K?
3. Which learners are sitting closest to the windows?
4. The scale of this plan is given as $1 : 50$. Using your ruler and calculator, calculate:
    * a) The width of the classroom (from the windows to the bookshelf).
    * b) The length of the classroom (from the blackboard to the wall behind learners M, N and O).
    * c) The width of the teacher's desk.
5. You sit at desk K. Explain how you would get from the door to your desk, using words like "left, right, in front of" and "behind".
6. Your friend forgets her bag in the classroom, and asks you to go and fetch it for her. She gives you the following directions: "Walk in through the door and turn right after the second row of desks. Walk past four rows of desks and look to your left, look over the desk next to you. My bag is on the floor." Where did she leave her bag?

---

#### Solutions

**1. Total Learners**
There are $15$ learners.

**2. Position relative to K**
Learner J.

**3. Proximity to windows**
Learners D, J and M.

**4. Scale Calculations**
Using the scale $1 : 50$, we multiply the measured length on the plan by $50$ to find the actual size.

*   **a) Width of the classroom:**
    $10,5\text{ cm} \times 50 = 525\text{ cm} = 5,25\text{ m}$
*   **b) Length of the classroom:**
    $15\text{ cm} \times 50 = 750\text{ cm} = 7,5\text{ m}$
*   **c) Width of the teacher's desk:**
    $4,5\text{ cm} \times 50 = 225\text{ cm} = 2,25\text{ m}$

**5. Directions to Desk K**
Walk in through the door and turn right before desk B. Walk straight down the aisle passing desks E and H on the left. At desk K (behind desk H) turn around to face the blackboard and sit down in the seat.

**6. Location of the bag**
Next to the bookshelf, or next to desk O.

# Activity 6: Using the Bar Scale and Directional Navigation

## Concept: Map Interpretation
To interpret a plan or map, you must understand how to use a **bar scale** to convert measurements on paper into real-world distances.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0017-00.png]**
> This graphic displays a table header for a data handling exercise, featuring the categorical variable "Gender" and the continuous numerical variables "Height (m)" and "Weight (kg)." It introduces the foundational CAPS concept of organizing raw data into structured formats, which is the essential first step for learners to perform statistical analysis and identify trends within a dataset.



### The Bar Scale
The bar scale provided on the plan is:
*   $0\text{ m}$ to $10\text{ m}$ represents a specific length on the page.
*   $10\text{ m}$ to $20\text{ m}$ represents the same length again.

To calculate real-world distances:
1.  Measure the distance on the map using a ruler (in $\text{mm}$).
2.  Determine how many "units" of the bar scale fit into your measured distance.
3.  Multiply the number of units by the scale value (e.g., $10\text{ m}$).

---

## Exercises

**1. Measure the width and length of the sports field in $\text{mm}$.**
*   *Instruction:* Use a standard ruler to measure the dimensions of the green rectangle labeled "Sports field" on the provided diagram.

**2. Use the bar scale to estimate the real (actual) width and length of the field in metres.**
*   *Method:* 
    *   Let $d_{map}$ be the distance measured on the map.
    *   Let $s_{map}$ be the length of the $10\text{ m}$ bar scale on the map.
    *   $\text{Actual distance} = \frac{d_{map}}{s_{map}} \times 10\text{ m}$

**3. What subject would you be studying if you are in classroom A3?**
*   *Instruction:* Locate "A3" on the plan and identify the category of classrooms it belongs to.

**4. What subject is taught in the classrooms that are next to the hall and face the sports fields?**
*   *Instruction:* Identify the block of classrooms labeled "B" and determine their subject based on the label provided on the plan.

**5. Tebogo, a new learner, has started at your school. You are in the Science classroom, A1, when the break bell rings. Explain to Tebogo how to get to the tuckshop.**
*   *Instruction:* Use directional language (e.g., North, South, East, West, left, right, past) to describe a path from A1 to the tuckshop.

---

For a high school CAPS student, this diagram illustrates how to interpret topographical data, analyse continuous quantitative relationships between distance and height, and calculate rates of change or gradients in real-world mathematical literacy and geography contexts.

### Worked Example 8: Understanding a Cinema Seating Plan

#### Concept: Interpreting Spatial Data
This exercise focuses on reading and interpreting a seating plan, which is a practical application of spatial reasoning and data analysis.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0018-00.png]**
> This graphic serves as a chapter heading for a mathematics textbook, featuring the text "Chapter 12" and the topic "Data Handling." It functions as an organizational signpost to introduce the CAPS curriculum module focused on the collection, organization, analysis, and interpretation of statistical data.



#### Legend
*   **Blue:** Available
*   **Red:** Booked
*   **Pink:** Unavailable
*   **Purple:** Wheelchair

---

#### Questions

1. If you wanted to book seats for a movie, which seats would you want to sit in?
2. Are seats N 11 and N 12 available?
3. Which seats offer you and your classmates a best view and why?
4. You are going to the movies with a friend in a wheelchair. Name one seat where they can sit, and the seat next to it where you can sit with them?
5. Where will you sit if you want to have a front view of the screen?
6. What fraction of row N has been booked?
7. What percentage of the theatre is still available?

---

#### Solutions

1. **Example answer:** L8 and L9.
2. **No.** They are booked (indicated by the red color).
3. **Middle section:** It provides a balanced perspective, being neither too close nor too far from the screen.
4. **Example answer:** Friend could sit in the wheelchair seat next to D1, and you could sit in D1.
5. **Row A.**
6. **Calculation:**
   *   Total seats in row N = 24
   *   Booked seats in row N (8, 9, 10, 11, 12) = 5
   *   Fraction = $\frac{5}{24}$
7. **Calculation:**
   *   Total available seats = 211
   *   Total seats in theatre = 234
   *   Percentage = $211 \div 234 \times 100 = 90,17\%$
   *   Rounded = $\approx 90\%$

# Lesson: Understanding Stadium Seating Plans

## Core Concepts
Stadium seating plans are spatial representations used to manage crowd control, ticket pricing, and navigation. Key elements include:
*   **Spatial Orientation:** Using landmarks (like the field, specific stands, or amenities) to describe relative positions.
*   **Categorization:** Grouping seats based on proximity to the action, comfort, or view, usually represented by a color-coded legend.
*   **Scale Drawings:** Using a ratio to represent large real-world dimensions on a smaller, manageable scale.

---

## Worked Example: Scale Drawings
To represent a large object on paper, we use a scale. The scale $1 : 1000$ means that $1\text{ cm}$ on the drawing represents $1000\text{ cm}$ ($10\text{ m}$) in reality.

**Problem:** Draw a rugby field ($70\text{ m}$ wide by $144\text{ m}$ long) using a scale of $1 : 1000$.

**Calculation:**
1.  **Convert real dimensions to centimeters:**
    *   Width: $70\text{ m} = 7000\text{ cm}$
    *   Length: $144\text{ m} = 14400\text{ cm}$

2.  **Apply the scale ($1 : 1000$):**
    *   Drawing Width: $\frac{7000}{1000} = 7\text{ cm}$
    *   Drawing Length: $\frac{14400}{1000} = 14.4\text{ cm}$

---

## Activity: Analyzing the Stadium Plan

### 1. Spatial Description (Point X)
Point X is located on the rugby field.
*   **Description:** The player is standing on the field, **near** the left try-line and **in the middle of** the field's width (between the top and bottom touchlines).

### 2. Purpose of Categorization
Seats are categorized to:
*   **Differentiate Pricing:** Premium views (closer to the center) cost more than restricted views.
*   **Manage Logistics:** Easier to direct crowds to specific entrances based on their ticket category.

### 3. Identifying Category 3 Seats
*   **Observation:** Category 3 (Red) is located in the "South Stand ramp seating" area.
*   **Position:** This stand is located at the bottom of the stadium plan, running horizontally along the length of the field.

### 4. Navigation (Point Y to South Stand Ramp)
*   **Route:** From Point Y (East side), move left along the East Stand, turn left at the corner, and proceed along the bottom of the stadium toward the South Stand ramp seating.

### 5. Locating Amenities (Point Z)
*   **Position:** Point Z is located in the top-left corner of the stadium, positioned behind the West Stand level 2 and to the left of the North Stand level 3.

### 6. Giving Directions
*   **Instruction:** "From the hot dog stand at Z, walk down past the West Stand (levels 2 and 1) toward the field. Turn left and walk along the bottom edge of the field until you reach the South Stand seating area. Look for Block A in the seating rows."

---

## Summary Table: Scale Drawing Dimensions

| Dimension | Real Size (m) | Real Size (cm) | Scale Drawing (cm) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Width** | $70$ | $7000$ | $7.0$ |
| **Length** | $144$ | $14400$ | $14.4$ |

### Worked Example 9: Finding our way in a shopping mall

**Question:** Study the map of the shopping centre below and answer the questions that follow:



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0020-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0020-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0020-01.png]**
> This graphic is a text-based heading labeled "Section 3:". It serves as a structural signpost within a CAPS-aligned educational resource to demarcate the beginning of a new topic or assessment module.



#### Questions

1. **Why do you think Checkers and Pick 'n Pay are the same colour?**
   *   **Answer:** They are likely both "anchor tenants" or large supermarkets, and the colour coding helps shoppers identify major stores quickly.

2. **Between which two shops would you find the escalator?**
   *   **Answer:** By observing the map, the escalator symbol is located between shop 41 and shop 40.

3. **If you want to go to the gym which entrance would you use? Explain why.**
   *   **Answer:** You would use the entrance near the "Gym" sign and the kiosk area. The map provides a directional arrow pointing toward the gym facility.

4. **Is the gym on the ground floor? Explain.**
   *   **Answer:** Yes, the gym is located on the main level of the shopping centre map, which represents the ground floor layout.

5. **If you are the owner of a store, what number shop would you choose for your shop? Explain why.**
   *   **Answer:** (Learner's choice). *Example:* I would choose a shop near the main entrances or near the large anchor stores (like Pick 'n Pay or Checkers) because these areas have the highest "foot traffic," meaning more potential customers will walk past my store.

### Map Reading and Navigation Exercises

#### Questions
6. Which entrance would you go to, to use a public phone?
7. By which entrances can disabled parking be found?
8. Give a possible reason for why this shopping centre has 4 ATM's.
9. Explain where the kiosks are situated.
10. You enter the shopping centre at entrance G3. You need to withdraw money from the ATM machine at shop number 32a. Give directions to explain how you would get there.

---

#### Solutions

| Question | Answer |
| :--- | :--- |
| **6** | G2. |
| **7** | Disabled parking can be found in the parking lots outside G2 and G3. There is no disabled parking outside G1. |
| **8** | Each ATM could be for a different bank - e.g. ABSA, FNB, Nedbank and Standard Bank. |
| **9** | The kiosks are situated in the middle of the mall between shops 5 and 39/40. They are near the main entrance to Edgars and the mall's information booth. |
| **10** | From entrance G3, go straight past shops 15, 14, 13, 12. At shop 20/21, turn right before the escalators. Keep going straight past Woolworths, and past the stairs and elevators (on your left). Pass shops 27, 28, 29, 30, 31 on your right. The ATM in front of shop 32a will be on your right, just before the public phones and entrance G2. |



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the beginning of the Data Handling unit within the CAPS mathematics curriculum.



*(Note: The following solutions refer to previous questions not shown on this specific page snippet)*

1. Because they are the same kind of store - they are both large supermarkets.
2. Between Edgars and shop 20/21.
3. You could use entrance G3 because it is closest to the escalators and stairs to the gym.
4. No. The gym is on the upper level. The gym logo points to the symbols for lifts and stairs, showing that it is up one level.
5. There are many possible answers to this, but one example is Shop 11a. Shop 11a is next door to the entrance to Pick n Pay, so there should be lots of people walking past it every day on their way to Pick n Pay. In other words, lots of people would see your shop and what you were selling! For similar reasons, Shop 36a next to Checkers would also be a good choice.

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-01.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-01.png]**

The diagram enables students to compare the central tendency and spread of height data between males and females, illustrating how grouped data can be visually represented to identify trends within a population.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-02.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0021-02.png)

In the context of the CAPS curriculum, this graphic identifies a chart format used to represent the composition of different categories by stacking sub-groups vertically to show both individual parts and the total whole.

### Activity 6 – 8: Navigating a Shopping Mall

#### Concept: Map Reading and Spatial Orientation
This activity focuses on interpreting a floor plan of a shopping centre. To navigate effectively, one must identify landmarks, understand spatial relationships (next to, opposite, near), and follow directional paths from specific entry points.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0022-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0022-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0022-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost for learners, indicating the commencement of the Data Handling unit within the CAPS mathematics curriculum.



---

#### Questions

1. **You want to go to Shop 37 to buy new shoes. What store will you find next to it?**
   *   *Instruction:* Locate Shop 37 on the map. Identify the adjacent shop numbers or names.

2. **What does “G51 Woolworths” mean on this map?**
   *   *Instruction:* Explain the naming convention used for the stores (e.g., "G" likely stands for Ground floor, followed by the specific unit number).

3. **Do you think this shopping centre has more than one floor? Explain your answer.**
   *   *Instruction:* Look for clues on the map that suggest vertical movement, such as symbols for stairs, escalators, or elevators.

4. **Where should you park if you want to go to Fournos bakery and buy some fresh bread?**
   *   *Instruction:* Locate "G40 Fournos bakery" and identify the nearest parking area indicated on the map.

5. **Name the two stores you could buy stationery from and describe how you would get to each of them from Entrance 1.**
   *   *Instruction:* Identify stores that typically sell stationery (e.g., CNA). Trace a path from Entrance 1 to these locations.

6. **If you are at Entrance 2, explain how you would get to the toilets.**
   *   *Instruction:* Locate Entrance 2 and the toilet icon. Describe the path through the corridors.

7. **You are standing at the entrance of Dis-Chem. Your friend arrives at Entrance 5 and wants to meet you. Give your friend directions to explain where they will find you.**
   *   *Instruction:* Use directional language (e.g., "walk past," "turn left," "head towards") to guide someone from Entrance 5 to the Dis-Chem (G14) area.

### 1. Scale Diagram Analysis
The provided diagram represents a room layout with a scale of $1 : 30$. This means that $1\text{ cm}$ on the drawing represents $30\text{ cm}$ in real life.

#### Questions

**a) Using the number scale, estimate the actual (real) size in metres of:**
*   i. The dining room table.
*   ii. The chairs.
*   iii. The cupboard.

**b) Using the number scale, estimate the actual (real) size of the dimensions of the room in metres.**

**c) Explain where the door to the room is.**

**d) Is it possible to calculate how high the windows are? Explain your answer.**

**e) Calculate the width of curtaining that will be required for both windows (in cm).**
*   *Note:* The width of the material required should be twice the width of the window.

**f) Rearrange the furniture in this room.**
*   To help you, you can redraw and cut out the same size shapes. 
*   Draw the basic structure of the room (the walls, windows, and door) in your books. 
*   Use the shapes you cut out to show your new room design. 
*   Paste them onto your room plan when you are happy with your design. 
*   You may also include some pictures from magazines to show what kind of furniture and accessories you would like in your room.

### Exercise: Scale, Maps, and Plans

#### 1. Carpet Scale Drawing
A carpet measures $1,8\text{ m}$ by $1,2\text{ m}$.

*   **i.** Using the scale $1 : 30$, draw a scale version of the carpet.
    *   *Calculation for length:* $\frac{1,8\text{ m}}{30} = 0,06\text{ m} = 6\text{ cm}$
    *   *Calculation for width:* $\frac{1,2\text{ m}}{30} = 0,04\text{ m} = 4\text{ cm}$
*   **ii.** Will the carpet fit into the room in Question 1? Explain your answer.

### 3. Scaled Dimensions and Mapping

**Task:** Calculate the scaled dimensions of a room and its furniture using a scale of $1 : 60$.

**Conversion Note:** Ensure all measurements are in the same unit (meters) before applying the scale.
*   $120\text{ cm} = 1,2\text{ m}$
*   $60\text{ cm} = 0,6\text{ m}$

**Calculation Formula:**
$$\text{Scaled Dimension} = \frac{\text{Actual Dimension}}{60}$$

| Item | Actual Dimension (m) | Scaled Dimension (cm) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Room Width | $3,6$ | $\frac{3,6}{60} \times 100 = 6$ |
| Room Length | $4,2$ | $\frac{4,2}{60} \times 100 = 7$ |
| Window | $1,2$ | $\frac{1,2}{60} \times 100 = 2$ |
| Door | $1,2$ | $\frac{1,2}{60} \times 100 = 2$ |
| Desk Width | $1,2$ | $\frac{1,2}{60} \times 100 = 2$ |
| Desk Length | $1,8$ | $\frac{1,8}{60} \times 100 = 3$ |
| Chair Width | $0,6$ | $\frac{0,6}{60} \times 100 = 1$ |
| Chair Length | $0,6$ | $\frac{0,6}{60} \times 100 = 1$ |
| Bookshelf | $1,5$ | $\frac{1,5}{60} \times 100 = 2,5$ |

### 4. The Hillvale Theatre Seating Analysis

**Ticket Details:**
| Detail | Information |
| :--- | :--- |
| Performance | The Hillvale Theatre |
| Seat | C 17 |
| Date | 15 May 2013 |
| Time | 20h00 |

**Questions:**
a) Why are there only 18 seats in row J?
b) Your ticket indicates that you must sit in C17. Which entrance would you use and why?
c) There are 3 seats to your right between you and your friend. What is your friend's seat number?
d) How many rows are between row B and row J?
e) How many seats are in the theatre?
f) Your school wants everyone to attend the stage production. If there are 30 people in your class, and the first person sits in seat D5, the class takes up the rest of row D (to D20) and seats from the beginning of row E (E1, E2 etc), where will the last person in your class sit in row E?
g) What percentage of the seats would your class of 30 fill?
h) Why are some rows labeled AA to FF?
i) Are rows AA and BB on the same level? Explain your answer.
j) Do you think the seats on the balcony are wheelchair accessible?
k) What ratio of the total number of seats are balcony seats?
l) How many seats will still be available if $\frac{4}{5}$ of the seats are sold?
m) The cost of the tickets for the stalls is R 200. If school groups get a $10\%$ discount, how much will your class's 30 tickets cost the school?

**Mathematical Guidance for (m):**
1. Calculate full price: $30 \times 200 = 6000$
2. Calculate discount: $6000 \times 0,10 = 600$
3. Calculate final cost: $6000 - 600 = 5400$

# Chapter 6: Scale, Maps, and Plans

## Introduction to Seating Plans
A seating plan is a diagrammatic representation of how seats are arranged in a venue, such as a theatre or stadium. These plans help in identifying seat locations, managing capacity, and organizing entry points.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0026-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0026-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0026-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook or digital resource to orient students to the specific curriculum section focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of data sets.



### Analysis of The Hillvale Theatre Seating Plan

The Hillvale Theatre is divided into two main sections: the **Stalls** (Rows A–J) and the **Balcony** (Rows AA–FF).

#### 1. The Stalls (Rows A–J)
The stalls are the ground-level seating area.
*   **Rows A to G:** Each row contains 20 seats.
*   **Rows H and J:** Each row contains 19 seats.
*   **Total capacity calculation:**
    *   Rows A–G: $7 \text{ rows} \times 20 \text{ seats} = 140 \text{ seats}$
    *   Rows H–J: $2 \text{ rows} \times 19 \text{ seats} = 38 \text{ seats}$
    *   **Total Stalls:** $140 + 38 = 178 \text{ seats}$

#### 2. The Balcony (Rows AA–FF)
The balcony is the elevated seating area.
*   **Rows AA and BB:** Each row contains 19 seats.
*   **Row CC:** Contains 14 seats.
*   **Row DD:** Contains 16 seats.
*   **Row EE:** Contains 5 seats.
*   **Row FF:** Contains 6 seats.
*   **Total capacity calculation:**
    *   $19 + 19 + 14 + 16 + 5 + 6 = 79 \text{ seats}$

---

### Summary Table: Theatre Capacity

| Section | Rows | Seats per Row | Total Seats |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Stalls | A–G | 20 | 140 |
| Stalls | H–J | 19 | 38 |
| Balcony | AA–BB | 19 | 38 |
| Balcony | CC | 14 | 14 |
| Balcony | DD | 16 | 16 |
| Balcony | EE | 5 | 5 |
| Balcony | FF | 6 | 6 |
| **Total** | | | **257** |

---

### Key Concepts for Learners
1.  **Spatial Orientation:** When reading a plan, identify the "Stage" to determine the front of the venue.
2.  **Access Points:** Note the locations of entrances (Entrance 1, 2, and 3) to understand the flow of traffic.
3.  **Data Extraction:** To find the total number of seats, multiply the number of rows by the seats per row if the rows are uniform, or sum the individual row totals if they vary.
4.  **Mathematical Application:** Use addition and multiplication to determine venue capacity, which is a fundamental skill in event planning and logistics.

### 5. Stadium Seating Plan Analysis



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0027-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a navigational signpost to introduce the mathematical statistics and data analysis module within the CAPS curriculum, signaling the transition to a unit focused on collecting, organizing, and interpreting numerical information.



#### Exercises

**a) Describe where the players would enter onto the field.**
The players enter the field through the tunnel located on the West side of the stadium.

**b) Are the blocks numbered in a clockwise or anti-clockwise direction?**
The blocks are numbered in an anti-clockwise direction.

**c) Your seat is in Block 35. What entrance would you go to to get to your seat?**
You would go to the South entrance.

**d) The south entrance is closest to Block 35. Where is this entrance?**
The South entrance is located at the bottom of the stadium diagram, near the purple seating blocks.

**e) How many blocks are there in the purple category?**
There are 18 blocks in the purple category (numbered 39 to 50 and 1 to 6).

**f) What percentage of the blocks are red?**
*   Total number of blocks = 71
*   Number of red blocks (12 to 19 and 51 to 59) = 17
*   Calculation: $\frac{17}{71} \times 100 \approx 23.94\%$

**g) If your friend is sitting in Block 25 and you are in one of the blue blocks. Describe where you are in relation to your friend.**
Block 25 is in the orange section (North side). The blue blocks (31 to 38) are located on the East side of the stadium. Therefore, you are to the right of your friend.

**h) Time Calculations**
*   **i. What time will the match finish?**
    *   Start time: 18:30
    *   Total duration: $80 \text{ minutes (game)} + 10 \text{ minutes (half-time)} = 90 \text{ minutes}$
    *   $90 \text{ minutes} = 1 \text{ hour } 30 \text{ minutes}$
    *   $18:30 + 1:30 = 20:00$
    *   The match will finish at 20:00.
*   **ii. The sun sets at 6:45 p.m. behind the main entrance. Where will you be sitting if you are looking into the sun during the game?**
    If the sun sets behind the main entrance (South), you would be looking into the sun if you are sitting in the North blocks (Orange/Yellow section).

**i) Ticket Prices**

| Category | Price |
| :--- | :--- |
| Orange | R250 |
| Green | R300 |
| Red | R450 |
| Purple | R200 |

*   **i. Which are the cheapest seats?**
    The cheapest seats are in the purple category at R200.
*   **ii. You are going to buy 3 tickets for yourself and your friends in the blue category. How much will this cost?**
    *   Price per blue ticket = R300
    *   Total cost = $3 \times R300 = R900$

### Map Reading and Spatial Reasoning

**6. Study the shopping mall map below and answer the questions that follow:**



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0028-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a navigational signpost to introduce the mathematical statistics and data analysis section of the CAPS curriculum.



#### Exercises

**a) What would you expect to be able to do in shop 148?**
*   **Answer:** Based on the label "Post office," you would be able to send mail, purchase stamps, or collect parcels.

**b) How many 'baby changing rooms' are there on the lower level and where are they?**
*   **Answer:** Locate the baby changing symbol (a person with a baby icon). There are 3 locations:
    1. Near the entrance to the corridor between Edgars and the Management office.
    2. Near the entrance to the corridor between Clicks and the Shoprite Checkers.
    3. Near the entrance to the corridor between Ackermans and the Jet/Pep area.

**c) Describe how you would get from Entrance 4 to Truworths.**
*   **Answer:** Enter through Entrance 4, walk past Mr Price (on your right), continue straight past Edgars, turn right at the Management office, and walk towards the escalator/stairs area where Truworths is located.

**d) Near which entrance would you park if you wanted to shop at Shoprite Checkers and Ackermans?**
*   **Answer:** Entrance 5.

**e) What would be a good meeting point for you and your friends? Explain your answer.**
*   **Answer:** A central, easily identifiable location such as the "Management" office or a specific shop entrance (e.g., the entrance to Edgars). These are good because they are central and easy to find on the map.

**f) Give three reasons why you would assume that this is a multi-level shopping centre.**
*   **Answer:**
    1. The map is explicitly labeled "LOWER LEVEL."
    2. There is an escalator symbol shown near the Truworths store.
    3. The presence of multiple entrances (4 and 5) at different sections of the building suggests a complex structure that likely spans more than one floor.

# Chapter 6: Scale, Maps, and Plans

This chapter focuses on the practical application of scale in representing real-world distances and spaces on paper.

---

## Chapter Contents

| Section | Topic | Page |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 6.2 | Number and bar scales | 126 |
| 6.3 | Maps, directions, seating and floor plans | 130 |
| 6.4 | End of chapter activity | 135 |

---

## Key Concepts

### 1. Number Scales
A number scale (or ratio scale) represents the relationship between a distance on a map and the corresponding distance in reality. It is expressed as a ratio:
$$1 : n$$
Where $1$ unit on the map represents $n$ units in the real world.

### 2. Bar Scales
A bar scale (or graphic scale) is a line segment drawn on a map that is divided into units. It allows for direct measurement of distances on the map to determine real-world distances without complex calculations.

### 3. Maps and Plans
*   **Maps:** Used to represent large geographical areas, showing locations, distances, and directions.
*   **Floor Plans:** Scaled diagrams of a room or building viewed from above, used to understand spatial layout and dimensions.
*   **Seating Plans:** Diagrams showing the arrangement of seats in a venue, often used to determine capacity or specific locations.

# 6. Scale, Maps and Plans

## 6.2 Number and Bar Scales

### Activity 6 – 1: Using the bar and number scales

#### 1. Number Scale Calculation
**Problem:** You measure the distance between two buildings on a map to be $5\text{ cm}$. If the map has a number scale of $1:100$, what is the actual distance on the ground?

**Solution:**
$$5\text{ cm} \times 100 = 500\text{ cm} = 5\text{ m}$$

---

#### 2. Number Scale Calculation
**Problem:** You are given a map with the number scale $1:20$. You measure a distance of $12\text{ cm}$ on the map. What is the actual distance in real life?

**Solution:**
$$12\text{ cm} \times 20 = 240\text{ cm} = 2,4\text{ m}$$

---

#### 3. Bar Scale Calculation
**Problem:** You measure a distance of $10\text{ cm}$ on a map with the following bar scale:



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0030-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0030-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0030-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the specific curriculum section focused on the collection, organization, and analysis of statistical information.



Given that $1\text{ cm} = 15\text{ m}$, what is the actual distance on the ground?

**Solution:**
$$10\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 10\text{ segments}$$
$$10\text{ segments} \times 15\text{ m} = 150\text{ m}$$

---

#### 4. Bar Scale Calculation
**Problem:** You measure a distance of $15\text{ cm}$ on a map with the following bar scale:



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0030-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0030-01.png)


Given that $2\text{ cm} = 100\text{ m}$, what is the actual distance on the ground?

**Solution:**
$$15\text{ cm} \div 2\text{ cm} = 7,5\text{ segments}$$
$$7,5\text{ segments} \times 100\text{ m} = 750\text{ m}$$

# Activity 6 – 2: Using the Number Scale

## Concept: Number Scales
A number scale (or ratio scale) represents the relationship between a distance on a map or plan and the actual distance in the real world. 

*   **Scale:** $1:500$
*   **Meaning:** $1$ unit on the map represents $500$ of the same units in reality.
*   **Conversion:** To convert centimetres ($\text{cm}$) to metres ($\text{m}$), divide by $100$:
    $$\text{Distance in m} = \frac{\text{Distance in cm}}{100}$$



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0031-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0031-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0031-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and the topic "Data Handling." It serves as an organizational signpost for learners, indicating the transition into the statistics and data analysis section of the CAPS curriculum.



---

## Worked Examples

### 1. The width and length of the school hall
*   **Measurement on map:** $5\text{ cm}$
*   **Calculation:** 
    $$5\text{ cm} \times 500 = 2500\text{ cm}$$
    $$\frac{2500\text{ cm}}{100} = 25\text{ m}$$
*   **Result:** The real width and length of the school hall is $25\text{ m}$.

### 2. The width of the toilet block
*   **Measurement on map:** $2\text{ cm}$
*   **Calculation:** 
    $$2\text{ cm} \times 500 = 1000\text{ cm}$$
    $$\frac{1000\text{ cm}}{100} = 10\text{ m}$$
*   **Result:** The real width of the toilet block is $10\text{ m}$.

### 3. The distance between the science and maths buildings
*   **Measurement on map:** $1\text{ cm}$
*   **Calculation:** 
    $$1\text{ cm} \times 500 = 500\text{ cm}$$
    $$\frac{500\text{ cm}}{100} = 5\text{ m}$$
*   **Result:** The real distance between the buildings is $5\text{ m}$.

![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0031-01.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0031-01.png)

### Activity 6 – 3: Using the bar scale to estimate actual length

Using the provided floor plan, you can calculate real-life dimensions by using the bar scale. The bar scale indicates that $1\text{ cm}$ on the diagram represents $20\text{ cm}$ in real life.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0032-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0032-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0032-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the specific section focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of numerical information within the CAPS curriculum.



---

#### 1. The length of the bookshelf
**Solution:**
*   Scale: $1\text{ cm}$ on the ruler $= 20\text{ cm}$ on the ground.
*   Measured width of bookshelf $= 7\text{ cm}$.
*   Number of segments $= 7\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 7$ segments.
*   Calculation: $7 \text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 140\text{ cm}$.
*   **Result:** The bookshelf is $1,4\text{ m}$ wide.

---

#### 2. The width and length of the chair
**Solution:**
*   Scale: $1\text{ cm}$ on the ruler $= 20\text{ cm}$ on the ground.
*   **Width:**
    *   Measured width $= 3,5\text{ cm}$.
    *   Number of segments $= 3,5\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 3,5$ segments.
    *   Calculation: $3,5 \text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 70\text{ cm}$.
*   **Length:**
    *   Measured length $= 4\text{ cm}$.
    *   Number of segments $= 4\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 4$ segments.
    *   Calculation: $4 \text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 80\text{ cm}$.
*   **Result:** The chair is $70\text{ cm}$ wide and $80\text{ cm}$ long.

---

#### 3. The length of each of the windows
**Solution:**
*   Scale: $1\text{ cm}$ on the ruler $= 20\text{ cm}$ on the ground.
*   **Left-hand window:**
    *   Measured length $= 5\text{ cm}$.
    *   Number of segments $= 5\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 5$ segments.
    *   Calculation: $5 \text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 100\text{ cm} = 1\text{ m}$.
*   **Bottom window:**
    *   Measured length $= 7\text{ cm}$.
    *   Number of segments $= 7\text{ cm} \div 1\text{ cm} = 7$ segments.
    *   Calculation: $7 \text{ segments} \times 20\text{ cm} = 140\text{ cm} = 1,4\text{ m}$.
*   **Result:** The left-hand window is $1\text{ m}$ long and the bottom window is $1,4\text{ m}$ long.

# Drawing a Scaled Map

## Concept: Using a Number Scale
When drawing a floor plan from real-life dimensions, you must convert all measurements to the same unit (usually centimetres) and then divide by the scale factor to determine the dimensions for your drawing.

### Activity 6 – 4: Drawing a Scaled Map

**Problem Statement:**
A bedroom measures $3,5\text{ m}$ by $4\text{ m}$. It contains a single bed measuring $92\text{ cm}$ by $188\text{ cm}$ and a bedside table that is $400\text{ mm}$ square. Draw a floor plan using a scale of $1 : 50$.



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0033-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient learners to the specific curriculum section focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of data sets.



---

### Worked Solution

#### 1. Room Real Measurements
First, convert all units to centimetres ($\text{cm}$):
*   Width: $3,5\text{ m} = 350\text{ cm}$
*   Length: $4\text{ m} = 400\text{ cm}$

**Scale Drawing Calculations (Scale $1 : 50$):**
*   Width: $350\text{ cm} \div 50 = 7\text{ cm}$
*   Length: $400\text{ cm} \div 50 = 8\text{ cm}$

#### 2. Bed Real Measurements
*   Width: $92\text{ cm}$
*   Length: $188\text{ cm}$

**Scale Drawing Calculations (Scale $1 : 50$):**
*   Width: $92\text{ cm} \div 50 = 1,84\text{ cm}$
*   Length: $188\text{ cm} \div 50 = 3,76\text{ cm}$

#### 3. Bedside Table Real Measurements
*   Dimensions: $400\text{ mm} \times 400\text{ mm}$
*   Convert to $\text{cm}$: $400\text{ mm} = 40\text{ cm}$

**Scale Drawing Calculations (Scale $1 : 50$):**
*   Side: $40\text{ cm} \div 50 = 0,8\text{ cm}$ (or $8\text{ mm}$)

# 6.3 Maps, Directions, Seating and Floor Plans



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0034-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0034-00.png)

## Activity 6 – 5: Drawing Scaled Maps

### Instructions
1. Divide into groups to measure and draw an accurate, scaled floor plan of your classroom.
2. Use a scale of $1 : 50$.
3. Measure all large objects (e.g., desks, windows, the blackboard) in the classroom.
4. Calculate the scaled dimensions for these objects.
5. Draw the objects carefully on your floor plan.
6. Consider if there is a different or better way to arrange the furniture in your classroom.

### Teacher's Note
* Provide square grid paper to learners to simplify the drawing process.
* Provide measuring tapes for learners to measure actual dimensions.
* If the classroom is large or contains many items, assign specific sections of the classroom to each group to manage the workload.

### Solution
* Learner-dependent answer.

# Activity 6: Using the Bar Scale and Directional Navigation on a School Ground Plan



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0035-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0035-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0035-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the specific curriculum section focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of data sets.



## Key Concepts
*   **Bar Scale:** A graphical representation of distance on a map or plan. It allows you to convert measurements taken on the paper into real-world distances.
*   **Scale Conversion:** Using the ratio provided by the bar scale to calculate actual dimensions.

---

## Worked Examples and Exercises

### 1. Measuring Dimensions
**Task:** Measure the width and length of the sports field in millimetres (mm).

**Solution:**
*   Width = $50\text{ mm}$
*   Length = $100\text{ mm}$

### 2. Calculating Actual Distance
**Task:** Use the bar scale to estimate the real (actual) width and length of the field in metres (m).

**Scale Reference:** $10\text{ mm}$ on the ruler = $10\text{ m}$ on the ground.

**Calculations:**

| Dimension | Measurement | Calculation | Actual Distance |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Width** | $50\text{ mm}$ | $\frac{50\text{ mm}}{10\text{ mm}} = 5\text{ segments} \times 10\text{ m}$ | $50\text{ m}$ |
| **Length** | $100\text{ mm}$ | $\frac{100\text{ mm}}{10\text{ mm}} = 10\text{ segments} \times 10\text{ m}$ | $100\text{ m}$ |

### 3. Navigation and Location
**Task:** What subject would you be studying if you are in classroom A3?

**Solution:**
*   Science (Classroom A3 is located in the Science classrooms block).

### 4. Spatial Analysis
**Task:** What subject is taught in the classrooms that are next to the hall and face the sports fields?

**Solution:**
*   Mathematics (Classrooms B1, B2, and B3).

### 5. Giving Directions
**Problem:** Tebogo, a new learner, has started at your school. You are in the Science classroom, A1, when the break bell rings. Explain to Tebogo how to get to the tuckshop.

**Solution:**
1. Walk out of the Science classroom towards the sports field.
2. Turn left and walk along the edge of the field towards the school hall building.
3. At the school hall, turn right.
4. At the first opportunity, turn left (before the maths classrooms) and walk past the maths building.
5. Then turn right and walk straight towards the tree.
6. At the tree, turn left. The tuckshop will be the building in front of you.

### Activity 6 – 7: Understanding a Stadium Seating Plan



![Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0036-00.png](images/Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0036-00.png)
> **[Diagram Context - Textbook_MathematicalLiteracy_Gr11_DATA-HANDLING.pdf-0036-00.png]**
> This graphic is a chapter heading banner containing the text "Chapter 12" and "Data Handling." It serves as a structural signpost in a textbook to orient students to the specific curriculum module focused on statistical analysis and the interpretation of quantitative information.



#### 1. Describing Position
**Question:** Using words such as "near to" and "in the middle of", describe the position of a player standing at the point marked X.

**Solution:**
A player at Point X would be standing on the field, near to the left-hand side goal posts, and in the middle of the field relative to the Category 5 seats in Block G of the South Stand ramp seating.

#### 2. Categorisation of Seats
**Question:** Why do you think the seats are categorised?

**Solution:**
Seats are categorised to:
*   **Differentiate pricing:** Seats with better views (e.g., central, lower levels) are usually more expensive than those with restricted views or further distances.
*   **Manage crowd control:** Categorisation helps in directing spectators to specific entrances and blocks.
*   **Organise logistics:** It assists stadium management in selling tickets and managing seating capacity for different types of events.

### Spatial Reasoning and Scale Drawings

#### Spatial Navigation Exercises
These exercises focus on interpreting maps, identifying positions, and providing clear directions within a stadium layout.

**3. Describe the position of the stand that contains the most Category 3 seats.**
*   **Solution:** The West Stand has the most Category 3 seats. It is located on the left side of the stadium, positioned between the North Stand and the South Stand.

**4. If you are standing at Point Y, what is the quickest way to get to the South Stand Ramp seating?**
*   **Solution:** Walk behind the East stand seating, keeping the stadium on your right. Then, turn right and walk straight to the South Stand Ramp seating.

**5. Describe the position of the hot dog stand at Point Z.**
*   **Solution:** The hot dog stand at Point Z is in the top-left corner of the diagram, situated between the West and North Stands.

**6. Your friend is at the hot dog stand (at Z). Explain to him how to find you if you are seated in a category 5 seat, Block A.**
*   **Solution:** Walk behind the West stands (keeping the stands on your left). Turn left at the end of the stands and walk straight to the edge of the South Stand ramp seating. Walk between the South Stand Ramp seating and South Stand Seating Level 1, passing Blocks H, G, F, E, D, C, and B. Block A (category 5) will be on your left, at the end of the South Stand Ramp seating.

---

#### Scale Drawings
To create a scale drawing, you must convert real-world measurements into smaller units using a specific ratio.

**7. The width of the rugby field is $70\text{ m}$ and the length is $144\text{ m}$. Draw a scale drawing of the rugby field using the number scale $1 : 1000$.**

*   **Step 1: Convert meters to centimeters.**
    *   $70\text{ m} = 70 \times 100 = 7000\text{ cm}$
    *   $144\text{ m} = 144 \times 100 = 14400\text{ cm}$

*   **Step 2: Apply the scale ($1 : 1000$).**
    *   Scaled Width: $7000\text{ cm} \div 1000 = 7\text{ cm}$
    *   Scaled Length: $14400\text{ cm} \div 1000 = 14,4\text{ cm}$

#### Exercises and Solutions

**1. You want to go to Shop 37 to buy new shoes. What store will you find next to it?**
*   **Solution:** Shop 35: CNA.

**2. What does “G51 Woolworths” mean on this map?**
*   **Solution:** It indicates that Woolworths is located at Shop 51 on the ground floor (the "G" stands for Ground floor).

**3. Do you think this shopping centre has more than one floor? Explain your answer.**
*   **Solution:** Yes. The map includes symbols for stairs, which indicates that there are other levels in the building.

**4. Where should you park if you want to go to Fournos bakery and buy some fresh bread?**
*   **Solution:** You should park near Entrance 5, as it is the closest entrance to the Fournos bakery (G40).

**5. Name the two stores you could buy stationery from and describe how you would get to each of them from Entrance 1.**
*   **Solution:**
    *   **Store 1 (CNA):** From Entrance 1, walk straight towards Shop 29. Turn right, then turn left around the corner at Shop 31. Continue straight; the CNA will be on your left.
    *   **Store 2 (Pick n Pay):** From Entrance 1, walk straight, passing shops G07 through G02 on your left. Turn left into the entrance of Pick n Pay.

### Navigation and Spatial Reasoning Exercises

#### Exercise 6: Directions from Entrance 2 to the Toilets
**Solution:**
1. Start at Entrance 2 and go straight.
2. Turn left at Shop 18, in front of the stairs.
3. Walk past shops 18–23 (following the curve of the mall to the right).
4. Turn slightly left towards Pick 'n Pay.
5. Just before Pick 'n Pay, turn right into the passageway between shops 28 and 29.
6. Go straight down this passageway; the toilets are located at the end.

#### Exercise 7: Directions from Entrance 5 to Dis-Chem
**Solution:**
1. Start at Entrance 5 and go straight, keeping to the left of the escalators in the middle.
2. Pass the entrance to Woolworths on your left.
3. Pass shops 53–56 (on your left).
4. Turn left in front of the escalators/stairs.
5. Go straight, passing shops G59, 58, and 57.
6. Dis-Chem will be directly in front of you.
